Universal Discovery and Invention: The Inventor's Notebook

JAH-UDI-000023 · The Inventor's Notebook

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for The Inventor's Notebook inside the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record discovery and invention as one connected workflow, from the first sight of an opportunity to a tested invention ready for construction. Discovery is finding what is true and not yet known; invention is making what is useful and does not yet exist; in practice they feed each other, because a discovery suggests an invention and an invention reveals new things to discover. Kept apart, each loses its context: discoveries pile up without use, and inventions are built without checking what is already known. Every record here specifies one step of the unified workflow completely enough that a researcher could follow it, a builder could implement it, and a checker could test whether it was done right.

Method: This record specifies The Inventor's Notebook as one working part of the Universal Discovery and Invention Center. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. Scope: The scope covers the unified workflow: opportunity scanning, problem framing, prior-knowledge search, hypothesis formation, experiment design, prototype invention, novelty checking, feasibility assessment, invention records, discovery logs, cross-domain transfer, serendipity capture, research planning, evidence collection, peer review inside the system, invention disclosure, iteration cycles, failure records, breakthrough criteria, technology readiness, invention-to-construction handover, discovery metrics, the inventor's notebook, and the discovery and invention lifecycle. Outside the scope are the subject contents of the discoveries and inventions themselves, which belong to their own subject databases, and the construction of finished inventions, which belongs to the creation and construction database. This database records the workflow; it does not duplicate the results. Requirements: Every workflow step in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name its inputs, including the prior knowledge that must be searched before new work begins. Second, it must name its outputs, including the record that proves the step happened. Third, it must be reproducible: another researcher following the same steps with the same inputs must reach the same result. Fourth, it must record failures as carefully as successes, because a failure record prevents the next researcher from repeating it. Fifth, it must check novelty against what already exists, so nothing is claimed as new that is not. Sixth, it must state its evidence plainly, with sources and dates. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: The workflow connects through four interfaces. The search interface queries the live research-discovery and systems-invention databases before new work begins, so prior knowledge is found first. The notebook interface records every observation, hypothesis, and test in dated order, so the path of the work can be replayed. The review interface sends each result to peer review inside the system, with the reviewers, the verdict, and the date recorded. The handover interface passes a tested invention to the creation and construction database with its specification, its evidence, and its open questions. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every handoff is a written record, so nothing is passed along by word of mouth. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for The Inventor's Notebook that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. 7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Discovery Librarian. It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Discovery Librarian).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss Universal Discovery and Invention Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.
